\[

\]

To determine the value of \( x \) for the point \((10, 14)\) that lies on a line perpendicular to the line passing through the points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\), we can follow these steps:

   The slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Plugging in the given points:
   \[
   m = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
   \]

2. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular line:**
   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope.
   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{3}} = -3
   \]

3. **Find the equation of the perpendicular line:**

   \[
   y = mx + b
   \]
   
   Substituting the known values:
   \[
   y = -3x + 8
   \]

4. **Plug in the given point and solve for \( x \):**

   Substitute \( y = 26 \) into the equation:
   \[
   26 = -3x + 8
   \]

   \[

   18 = -3x \\
   x = \frac{18}{-3} \\
   x = -6

To determine the **midpoint** of the line segment connecting the points \( P(2, 2) \) and \( B(10, 2) \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Formula for Midpoint
The midpoint \( M \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]

### Step 2: Apply the Values to the Formula
Given:
- \( P(x_1, y_1) = (2, 2) \)
- \( B(x_2, y_2) = (10, 2) \)

Plugging these into the midpoint formula:
\[
M_x = \frac{2 + 10}{2} = \frac{12}{2} = 6
\]
\[
M_y = \frac{2 + 2}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]

### Step 3: Determine the Midpoint Coordinates
Thus, the midpoint \( M \) is:
\[
M = (6,\ 2)
\]

### Final Answer
The midpoint of the line segment connecting points \( P(2, 2) \) and \( B(10, 2) \) is:

To find the slopes of the **perpendicular** (right angle) lines to the line passing through the points \((4, 4)\) and \((3, 5)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Original Line
The slope (\(m\)) of a line passing through two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is calculated using:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{5 - 4}{3 - 4} = \frac{1}{-1} = -1
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of Perpendicular Lines
The slope of a line **perpendicular** to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope.

For our case:
\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -\frac{1}{-1} = 1
\]

### Final Answer
Both lines that form right angles with the line passing through \((4, 4)\) and \((3, 5)\) have a slope of \(1\).

**Final Answer**
The slope of the line is \boxed{\pm \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{3}}.

To determine the slope of a line that makes an angle of 60 degrees with the Y-axis, we need to consider the relationship between angles from the X-axis and the Y-axis.

1. A line making an angle of 60 degrees with the positive Y-axis can be either towards the left (negative X direction) or towards the right (positive X direction).
2. If the line is towards the left, the angle it makes with the positive X-axis is 150 degrees (since 90 + 60 = 150). The slope is calculated as:
   \[
   \tan(150^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 30^\circ) = -\tan(30^\circ) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}
   \]
3. If the line is towards the right, the angle it makes with the positive X-axis is 30 degrees (since 90 - 60 = 30). The slope is calculated as:
   \[
   \tan(30^\circ) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}
   \]

Thus, considering both possibilities, the slope of the line can be either positive or negative.

\[
\boxed{\pm \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{3}}
\]

To determine whether the point \((-3, 2)\) has a special relationship with the given points using the midpoint formula, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Given Points**:
   - Let's assume we have three distinct points: \(A(-2, 0)\), \(B(3, 3)\), and an additional point \(C\) which might be a duplicate or another coordinate.

2. **Calculate Midpoints**:
   - To apply the midpoint formula between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\), use:
     \[
     M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
     \]
   - Example: Midpoint between \(A(-2, 0)\) and \(B(3, 3)\):
     \[
     M = \left( \frac{-2 + 3}{2}, \frac{0 + 3}{2} \right) = \left( 0.5, 1.5 \right)
     \]

3. **Check for Equidistance**:
   - To see if \(C(-3, 2)\) is equidistant from \(A\) and \(B\), calculate the distances using the distance formula:
     \[
     d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
     \]
   - Distance between \(C(-3, 2)\) and \(A(-2, 0)\):
     \[
     d_{CA} = \sqrt{(-3 + 2)^2 + (2 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{1 + 4} = \sqrt{5}
     \]
   - Distance between \(C(-3, 2)\) and \(B(3, 3)\):
     \[
     d_{CB} = \sqrt{(3 + 3)^2 + (3 - 2)^2} = \sqrt{36 + 1} = \sqrt{37}
     \]
   - Since \(d_{CA} \neq d_{CB}\), the point \((-3, 2)\) is not equidistant from points \(A\) and \(B\).

4. **Conclusion**:
   - Based on these calculations, the point \((-3, 2)\) does not have a special relationship (such as being equidistant or lying on a line passing through midpoints) with the given points using the midpoint formula.

Answer: The point \((-3, 2)\) is not equidistant from \((-2, 0)\) and \((3, 3)\), so it does not have the special relationship described.

To determine the **slope** of the line that passes through the points \((5, 1)\) and \((6, 0)\), we use the formula for the slope \(m\) between two points:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Calculation:**

1. **Identify the coordinates:**
   \[
   (x_1, y_1) = (5, 1)
   \]
   \[
   (x_2, y_2) = (6, 0)
   \]

2. **Apply the slope formula:**
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{0 - 1}{6 - 5}
   \]

3. **Calculate the differences:**
   \[
   y_2 - y_1 = 0 - 1 = -1
   \]
   \[
   x_2 - x_1 = 6 - 5 = 1
   \]

4. **Compute the slope:**
   \[
   m = \frac{-1}{1} = -1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\text{Slope } (m) = -1}
\]

The problem involves finding the slopes of two lines where one slope is twice the other, given that their tangent (θ) between them has a value of 1/3. 

Let’s denote the smaller angle's slope as \( m \), so the larger angle's slope is \( 2m \).

Using the formula for the tangent between two lines:
\[
\tan \theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]
Substituting \( m_2 = 2m_1 = 2m \), we get:
\[
\frac{1}{3} = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + (2m)(m)} \right| = \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right|
\]
This simplifies to two equations considering the absolute value:
1. \( \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} = \frac{1}{3} \)
2. \( \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} = -\frac{1}{3} \)

Solving both cases:

**Case 1:**
\[
3m = 1 + 2m^2 \\
2m^2 - 3m + 1 = 0
\]
Quadratic formula gives \( m = 1 \) or \( m = \frac{1}{2} \).

**Case 2:**
\[
3m = -(1 + 2m^2) \\
2m^2 + 3m + 1 = 0
\]
Quadratic formula gives \( m = -1 \) or \( m = -\frac{1}{2} \).

Thus, the possible pairs of slopes are:
- \( (1, 2) \)
- \( \left( \frac{1}{2}, 1 \right) \)
- \( (-1, -2) \)
- \( \left( -\frac{1}{2}, -1 \right) \)

However, each pair is essentially the same when considering lines' slopes because swapping gives the reverse. So the unique solutions are:
**Final Answer:**
The possible pairs of slopes are 1 and 2, or -1 and -2.

\boxed{\left\{ (1, 2), (-1, -2) \right\}}

Given that the slope (m) is -2, the equation becomes y = -2x + b. 

To find the value of b, I substitute the coordinates of the given point (0, 5) into the equation. Plugging in x = 0 and y = 5 gives me:

This simplifies to 5 = 0 + b, so b = 5.

**Solution:**

We need to find the equation of a straight line with a slope (gradient) of \(-2\) that passes through the point \(A(0, 5)\).

The general form of the equation of a straight line is:
\[ y = mx + c \]
where:
- \(m\) is the slope,
- \(c\) is the y-intercept.

**Step 1: Identify the known values**
- Slope (\(m\)) = \(-2\)
- Point through which the line passes: \(A(0, 5)\)

**Step 2: Substitute the known values into the equation**

\[

5 = 0 + c \\
c = 5
\]

   - The equation of a line is \( y = mx + b \).

3. **Equation of the line:**
   - Thus, the equation is \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2 \).

Given the vertices of triangle \(PQR\) at points \(P(2,1)\), \(Q(-2,3)\), and \(R(4,5)\), we need to find the equations of the three altitudes.

1. **Altitude from point \(Q(-2,3)\)**:
   - The side \(PR\) has a slope calculated as follows: 
     \[
     \text{slope of } PR = \frac{5 - 1}{4 - 2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
     \]
   - The altitude from \(Q\) is perpendicular to \(PR\), so its slope is the negative reciprocal, which is \(-\frac{1}{2}\).
   - Using the point-slope form with point \(Q(-2,3)\):
     \[
     y - 3 = -\frac{1}{2}(x + 2)
     \]
     Multiplying through by 2 to clear the fraction:
     \[
     2(y - 3) = - (x + 2) \implies 2y - 6 = -x - 2 \implies x + 2y = 4
     \]
   - The equation of the altitude from \(Q\) is \(\boxed{x + 2y = 4}\).

2. **Altitude from point \(R(4,5)\)**:
   - The side \(PQ\) has a slope calculated as follows:
     \[
     \text{slope of } PQ = \frac{3 - 1}{-2 - 2} = \frac{2}{-4} = -\frac{1}{2}
     \]
   - The altitude from \(R\) is perpendicular to \(PQ\), so its slope is the negative reciprocal, which is \(2\).
   - Using the point-slope form with point \(R(4,5)\):
     \[
     y - 5 = 2(x - 4)
     \]
     Simplifying:
     \[
     y = 2x - 8 + 5 \implies y = 2x - 3
     \]
   - The equation of the altitude from \(R\) is \(\boxed{2x - y = 3}\).

3. **Altitude from point \(P(2,1)\)**:
   - The side \(QR\) has a slope calculated as follows:
     \[
     \text{slope of } QR = \frac{5 - 3}{4 - (-2)} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
     \]
   - The altitude from \(P\) is perpendicular to \(QR\), so its slope is the negative reciprocal, which is \(-3\).
   - Using the point-slope form with point \(P(2,1)\):
     \[
     y - 1 = -3(x - 2)
     \]
     Simplifying:
     \[
     y = -3x + 6 + 1 \implies y = -3x + 7
     \]
   - The equation of the altitude from \(P\) is \(\boxed{3x + y = 7}\).

Thus, the equations of the three altitudes are:
\[
\boxed{x + 2y = 4}, \boxed{2x - y = 3}, \text{ and } \boxed{3x + y = 7}
\]

To determine the distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x - 6y -28 = 0, I start by identifying the general form of a linear equation, which is Ax + By + C = 0. Here, A is 5, B is -6, and C is -28.

Next, I use the distance formula from a point to a line: 

\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Plugging in the values:

\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|5(5) - 6(-7) -28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}} \]

Calculating the numerator:

\[ |25 + 42 - 28| = |39| = 39 \]

Calculating the denominator:

\[ \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61} \]

Therefore, the distance is \( \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} \).

To find the distance from the point (5, -7) to the line given by the equation \(5x - 6y -28 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the coefficients**:
   - The general form of the line is \(Ax + By + C = 0\).
   - Here, \(A = 5\), \(B = -6\), and \(C = -28\).

2. **Use the distance formula**:
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]
   Where \((x_0, y_0) = (5, -7)\).

3. **Plug in the values**:
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}} 
   \]
   
4. **Calculate the numerator**:
   \[
   |25 + 42 - 28| = |39| = 39
   \]

5. **Calculate the denominator**:
   \[
   \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]
   
6. **Final distance calculation**:
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
   \]

### Final Answer: {Final Answer: "39/sqrt(61)"}

</think>

To find the angle between the two lines given by their direction vectors, we start with the vectors \( \mathbf{u} = (1, 3) \) and \( \mathbf{v} = (1, -\sqrt{3}) \).

First, we calculate the dot product of the vectors:
\[ \mathbf{u} \cdot \mathbf{v} = 1 \cdot 1 + 3 \cdot (-\sqrt{3}) = 1 - 3\sqrt{3} \]

Next, we find the magnitudes of the vectors:
\[ |\mathbf{u}| = \sqrt{1^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{10} \]
\[ |\mathbf{v}| = \sqrt{1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2} = \sqrt{4} = 2 \]

Using the dot product formula to find the cosine of the angle \( \theta \) between the vectors:
\[ \cos(\theta) = \frac{\mathbf{u} \cdot \mathbf{v}}{|\mathbf{u}| |\mathbf{v}|} = \frac{1 - 3\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{10} \cdot 2} \]

We approximate the value of \( 1 - 3\sqrt{3} \):
\[ 1 - 3\sqrt{3} \approx 1 - 5.196 = -4.196 \]
Taking the absolute value for cosine:
\[ |\cos(\theta)| = \left| \frac{-4.196}{2\sqrt{10}} \right| \approx \left| \frac{-4.196}{6.324} \right| \approx 0.663 \]

Thus, we find:
\[ \theta = \arccos(0.663) \approx 48^\circ \]

To confirm, we use the cross product to find the sine of the angle:
\[ |\mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{v}| = |1 \cdot (-\sqrt{3}) - 3 \cdot 1| = |-\sqrt{3} - 3| = \sqrt{3} + 3 \]
\[ \sin(\theta) = \frac{\sqrt{3} + 3}{2\sqrt{10}} \approx \frac{4.732}{6.324} \approx 0.748 \]

Thus, we find:
\[ \theta = \arcsin(0.748) \approx 48^\circ \]

Both methods confirm that the angle between the lines is approximately \( 48^\circ \).

The acute angle between the two lines is \boxed{48^\circ}.

So the slope (m) of the given line is 1/7.

y + 3 = -7x + 35

Which simplifies to:
7x + y - 32 = 0

So the equation of the perpendicular line is 7x + y - 32 = 0.

To find the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the line \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Rewrite the Equation in Standard Form
Start by expanding and rearranging the given equation into the standard linear form \(Ax + By + C = 0\).

\[
\begin{align*}
24(x + 12) &= 10(y - 4) \\
24x + 288 &= 10y - 40 \\
24x - 10y + 328 &= 0
\end{align*}
\]

### Step 2: Identify Coefficients
From the equation \(24x - 10y + 328 = 0\), identify the coefficients:
- \(A = 24\)
- \(B = -10\)
- \(C = 328\)

### Step 3: Apply the Distance Formula
The distance \(d\) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is given by:

\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Plug in the values:
- \(x_0 = -2\)
- \(y_0 = 2\)

\[
\begin{align*}
d &= \frac{|24(-2) + (-10)(2) + 328|}{\sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2}} \\
&= \frac{|-48 - 20 + 328|}{\sqrt{576 + 100}} \\
&= \frac{|260|}{\sqrt{676}} \\
&= \frac{260}{26} \\
&= 10
\end{align*}
\]

### Final Answer

\boxed{\text{Final Answer: "10"}}

To find the distance from the point (1,2) to the line \(4x +7y +5 =0\), we use the formula:

Distance = \(\frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}\)

Where \(A=4\), \(B=7\), \(C=5\), and \((x_0, y_0) = (1, 2)\).

Calculating the numerator:

\( |4*1 + 7*2 + 5| = |4 + 14 + 5| = |23| = 23 \)

Denominator:

\(\sqrt{4^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{16 + 49} = \sqrt{65}\)

So the distance is \( \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}} \). Rationalizing the denominator gives \( \frac{23\sqrt{65}}{65} \).

Additionally, checking if (1,2) lies on the second line \(2x - y =0\):

Substituting x=1 and y=2: \(2*1 - 2 =0\) which is true. Hence, the distance from (1,2) to this line is zero.

But since only the first line was specified for calculation, the main answer is:

\(\boxed{\dfrac{23}{\sqrt{65}}}\)

Or in rationalized form: \(\boxed{\dfrac{23\sqrt{65}}{65}}\)

d = |a*x₀ + b*y₀ + c| / sqrt(a² + b²)

Okay, so let's identify the coefficients in our equation. The given line is 4x - y = 0. Comparing this with ax + by + c = 0, I see that a = 4, b = -1, and c = 0.

Now, plugging in the values of the point (4, 1) into the formula:

Numerator: |4*4 + (-1)*1 + 0| = |16 - 1| = |15| = 15

Denominator: sqrt(4² + (-1)²) = sqrt(16 + 1) = sqrt(17)

The distance from the point \( (4, 1) \) to the line \( 4x - y = 0 \) can be calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line. 

Given:
- Line equation: \( 4x - y = 0 \)
- Point: \( (4, 1) \)

The formula is:
\[
d = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]
Here, \( a = 4 \), \( b = -1 \), and \( c = 0 \).

Plugging in the values:
\[
d = \frac{|4(4) - 1(1) + 0|}{\sqrt{4^2 + (-1)^2}} = \frac{|16 - 1|}{\sqrt{16 + 1}} = \frac{15}{\sqrt{17}}
\]
Rationalizing the denominator:
\[
d = \frac{15}{\sqrt{17}} \times \frac{\sqrt{17}}{\sqrt{17}} = \frac{15\sqrt{17}}{17}
\]

The final distance is \( \frac{15\sqrt{17}}{17} \).

I need to determine the slope of a line that passes through two given points: (6, -4) and (-2, 8).

First, I'll recall the formula for calculating the slope between two points. The formula is:

slope = (y₂ - y₁) / (x₂ - x₁)

Next, I'll assign the coordinates to their respective variables:
x₁ = 6
y₁ = -4
x₂ = -2
y₂ = 8

Now, I'll substitute these values into the formula.

Calculate the difference in the y-values: 8 - (-4) equals 12.
Then, calculate the difference in the x-values: -2 - 6 equals -8.

Finally, I'll divide the change in y by the change in x to find the slope:
slope = 12 / (-8) = -3/2

So, the slope of the line is -3⁄2.
</think>

**Solution:**  
To determine the slope \( m \) of the line passing through the points \( (6, -4) \) and \( (-2, 8) \), we use the formula:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Calculation:**  
1. **Identify the coordinates:**
   \[
   (x_1, y_1) = (6, -4), \quad (x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)
   \]
   
2. **Apply the slope formula:**
   \[
   m = \frac{8 - (-4)}{-2 - 6} = \frac{8 + 4}{-8} = \frac{12}{-8}
   \]

3. **Simplify the fraction:**
   \[
   m = \frac{12}{-8} = \frac{-3}{2}
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\text{Final Answer: "-3/2"}}

The problem involves finding the slope(s) of a second line that forms an angle of π/4 radians with another line having a slope of 1/4. Using the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines, we set up the equation and solve for the unknown slope.

**Solution:**

Given:
- Slope of the first line, \( m_1 = \frac{1}{4} \)
- Angle between the lines, \( \theta = \frac{\pi}{4} \), so \( \tan\theta = 1 \)

Using the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines:
\[ \tan\theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right| \]

Substitute the known values:
\[ 1 = \left| \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} \right| \]

This results in two possible equations due to the absolute value:

**Case 1:**
\[ \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = 1 \]
Solving this gives:
\[ m_2 = \frac{5}{3} \]

**Case 2:**
\[ \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = -1 \]
Solving this gives:
\[ m_2 = -\frac{3}{5} \]

Both slopes are valid as they satisfy the given angle condition.

To solve for \( X \), we'll follow these steps:

**Step 1: Identify the given points**
- Point A: (0, 8)
- Point B: (6, 10)

**Step 2: Calculate the slope of line AB**
\[
\text{Slope}_{AB} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 3: Determine the slope of the perpendicular line**
The slope of a line perpendicular to AB is the negative reciprocal:
\[
\text{Slope}_{\perp} = -\frac{1}{\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)} = -3
\]

**Step 4: Use point-slope form with the given point (10, 14)**
The equation of the perpendicular line is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substitute \( m = -3 \), \( x_1 = 10 \), and \( y_1 = 14 \):
\[
y - 14 = -3(x - 10)
\]

**Step 5: Simplify the equation to find where it intersects (x, 26)**
Substitute \( y = 26 \) into the equation:
\[
26 - 14 = -3(x - 10)
\]
Simplify:
\[
12 = -3x + 30
\]
Rearrange to solve for \( x \):
\[
-18 = -3x \\
x = 6
\]

\[
\boxed{6}

Similarly, the distance from (a,0) to (5,6): sqrt[(5 - a)^2 + (6 - 0)^2]
That's sqrt[(5 - a)^2 + 36]

Since these distances are equal, I can set them equal and solve for 'a':

Right: 25 + 36 -10a + a^2 = 61 -10a + a^2

**Solution:**

We are given two points \( P(2, 2) \) and \( Q(10, 2) \). We need to find the slope of the line segment connecting these two points and then determine its midpoint.

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope
The formula for the slope \( m \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given values:
\[
m = \frac{2 - 2}{10 - 2} = \frac{0}{8} = 0
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Midpoint
The midpoint \( M \) of a line segment with endpoints \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the given values:
\[
M_x = \frac{2 + 10}{2} = \frac{12}{2} = 6
\]
\[
M_y = \frac{2 + 2}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
So, the midpoint is \( (6, 2) \).

### Final Answer:
The slope of the line segment is \( 0 \), and its midpoint is at \( (6, 2) \).

\[
\boxed{
\text{Slope: } 0,\quad \text{Midpoint: }(6, 2)
}
\]

The possible slopes are 2 and 1, or -2 and -1. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Define Variables:** Let’s denote the two lines as having slopes \( m \) and \( n \), with one being twice the other, so let’s say \( m = 2n \).

2. **Tangent of Angle Between Lines Formula:** The tangent of the angle \( \theta \) between two lines is given by:
   \[
   \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m - n}{1 + mn} \right|
   \]
   
3. **Set Up Equation:** Given that \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{3} \), substitute into the formula and set up the equation:
   \[
   \left| \frac{2n - n}{1 + 2n^2} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
   \]
   
4. **Simplify:** This simplifies to:
   \[
   \left| \frac{n}{1 + 2n^2} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
   \]
   
5. **Remove Absolute Value:** Consider both positive and negative cases, leading to two equations:
   - \( n/(1 + 2n^2) = 1/3 \)
   - \( n/(1 + 2n^2) = -1/3 \)

6. **Solve for \( n \):** Cross-multiplied and rearranged, this becomes a cubic equation in terms of \( n \).

7. **Find Solutions:** Solving the cubic gives possible values for \( n \), which are \( n = 1, -1, 0.5, -0.5 \). Thus, corresponding to these \( n \) values, \( m = 2n \) will be \( 2, -2, 1, -1 \).

8. **Possible Pairs:** The possible pairs of slopes are:
   - When \( n = 1 \), \( m = 2 \): Pair (2, 1)
   - When \( n = -1 \), \( m = -2 \): Pair (-2, -1)
   - When \( n = 0.5 \), \( m = 1 \): Pair (1, 0.5)
   - When \( n = -0.5 \), \( m = -1 \): Pair (-1, -0.5)

9. **Conclusion:** The slopes are such that each pair consists of a number and its double (or half). The valid slope pairs where one is twice the other and satisfy the given tangent condition are (2, 1) and (-2, -1).

**Final Answer:**
The possible slopes are \( \boxed{2} \) and \( \boxed{1} \), or \( \boxed{-2} \) and \( \boxed{-1} \).

First, I identify that two points are given: (3, 1) and (5, 7). To find the equation of the line passing through these points, I'll start by calculating the slope.

Let's find the equation of the line passing through the points \((3, 1)\) and \((5, 7)\).

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope (m)
The formula for the slope \(m\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

### Step 2: Find the Y-Intercept (b)
Using the slope-intercept form \(y = mx + b\) and one of the given points, let's solve for \(b\). We'll use the point \((3, 1)\):
\[
1 = 3(3) + b
\]
\[
1 = 9 + b
\]
\[
b = 1 - 9 = -8
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Line
Substituting \(m = 3\) and \(b = -8\) into the slope-intercept form:
\[
y = 3x - 8
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the line is:
\[
\boxed{y = 3x - 8}
\]

Using the slope formula:
m = (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁)
Plugging in the values:
m = (-2 - 0)/(-2 - 3) = (-2)/(-5) = 2/5

Now, I'll use the point-slope form to find the equation of the line. Using point (3, 0):
y - y₁ = m(x - x₁)
y - 0 = (2/5)(x - 3)
Simplifying:
y = (2/5)x - 6/5

Next, I'll verify if the third point (8, 2) lies on this line by substituting its coordinates into the equation.

Calculating the right side with x = 8:
(2/5)(8) - 6/5 = 16/5 - 6/5 = 10/5 = 2

To determine if the points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are collinear (lie on the same straight line), we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Equation of the Line Passing Through Two Points
Let's choose two points to find the equation of the line. We'll use \((3, 0)\) and \((-2, -2)\).

**Calculate the Slope (\(m\)):**
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
\]

**Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation:**
Using point \((3, 0)\):
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \\
y - 0 = \frac{2}{5}(x - 3) \\
y = \frac{2}{5}x - \frac{6}{5}
\]

So, the equation of the line is:
\[
y = \frac{2}{5}x - \frac{6}{5}
\]

### Step 2: Verify if the Third Point Lies on This Line
Now, check if the third point \((8, 2)\) satisfies this equation.

**Substitute \(x = 8\) into the Equation:**
\[
y = \frac{2}{5}(8) - \frac{6}{5} \\
y = \frac{16}{5} - \frac{6}{5} \\
y = \frac{10}{5} \\
y = 2
\]

Since \(y = 2\) matches the \(y\)-coordinate of the third point, it lies on the line.

### Conclusion
All three points lie on the same straight line.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\text{Yes, the points are collinear.}}
\]

Given the problem of finding the new break-even points when producing both products P1 and P2 together with a decrease in fixed costs, we need to follow these steps:

1. **Identify the original contribution margins for each product:**
   - Contribution margin for P1: \( 50 - 30 = 20 \) dollars per unit.
   - Contribution margin for P2: \( 40 - 20 = 20 \) dollars per unit.

2. **New fixed costs when producing both products together:**
   - Original fixed costs were $60,000. When producing both products together, fixed costs decrease by 25%, so the new fixed cost is:
     \[
     60,000 \times (1 - 0.25) = 45,000 \text{ dollars}
     \]

3. **Calculate the total contribution needed to break even:**
   - The total contribution required to break even is equal to the new fixed costs:
     \[
     45,000 \text{ dollars}
     \]

4. **Determine the total units needed to be sold:**
   - Since each product contributes $20 per unit, the total number of units needed to break even is:
     \[
     \frac{45,000}{20} = 2,250 \text{ units}
     \]

Thus, the new break-even point in terms of total units sold when producing both products together is:

\[
\boxed{2250}
\]

The equation of the line passing through (0, 2) and making an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians with the positive x-axis has a slope of \( -\sqrt{3} \). Therefore, its equation is:

\( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2 \)

For a parallel line, it will have the same slope but a different y-intercept. Let this y-intercept be \( c \). Thus, the equation of such a line is:

\( y = -\sqrt{3}x + c \)

Alright, so I have this problem here where I need to find the equation of a line that passes through two given points. The points are (4, 4) and (-13, 13). Hmm, okay, let me think about how to approach this step by step.

First off, I remember that to find the equation of a line when you have two points, you can use the slope-intercept form or the point-slope form. But maybe starting with finding the slope would be better because it's usually a good starting point.

So, let me recall the formula for slope (m). The slope between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is given by m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1). Plugging in the values from my points: (4, 4) as (x1, y1) and (-13, 13) as (x2, y2).

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- Points \( P(2, 1) \), \( Q(-2, 3) \)
- The perpendiculars at vertices \( P \) and \( Q \) intersect at vertex \( R \)

**Objective:**
Find the coordinates of vertex \( R \).

---

### Step 1: Determine the Slopes of Perpendicular Lines

Assume that δPQR has a right angle at vertex \( R \). This means:
- The line through \( P \) and perpendicular to QR has slope \( m_1 \).
- The line through \( Q \) and perpendicular to PR has slope \( m_2 \).

However, since the problem states that both perpendiculars (at \( P \) and \( Q \)) intersect at vertex \( R \), we can use the following approach:

---

### Step 2: Equation of Perpendicular from \( P(2, 1) \)

Assume the slope of line QR is \( m_1 \). Then, the slope of the perpendicular to QR through \( P \) will be \( -\frac{1}{m_1} \).

But without additional information, we need more constraints. For simplicity, let's assume that the slopes are given or can be derived.

Let’s suppose the slope of line QR is 3 (for illustration purposes). Then:
- The perpendicular to QR through \( P(2, 1) \) will have a slope of \( -\frac{1}{3} \).

Using point-slope form:
\[ y - 1 = -\frac{1}{3}(x - 2) \]
Multiply both sides by 3 to eliminate the fraction:
\[ 3(y - 1) = -(x - 2) \]
Simplify:
\[ 3y - 3 = -x + 2 \]
Rearrange terms:
\[ x + 3y = 5 \]

---

### Step 3: Equation of Perpendicular from \( Q(-2, 3) \)

Similarly, suppose the slope of line PR is such that its perpendicular has a certain slope.

For simplicity, let's assume the slope of PR is -1. Then:
- The perpendicular to PR through \( Q(-2, 3) \) will have a slope of 1.

Using point-slope form:
\[ y - 3 = 1(x + 2) \]
Simplify:
\[ y - 3 = x + 2 \]
Rearrange terms:
\[ x - y = -5 \]

---

### Step 4: Solve the Two Equations to Find \( R \)

We have two equations:
1. \( x + 3y = 5 \)
2. \( x - y = -5 \)

Subtract equation 2 from equation 1:
\[ (x + 3y) - (x - y) = 5 - (-5) \]
Simplify:
\[ 4y = 10 \]
\[ y = \frac{10}{4} = \frac{5}{2} \]

Substitute \( y = \frac{5}{2} \) into equation 2:
\[ x - \frac{5}{2} = -5 \]
\[ x = -5 + \frac{5}{2} \]
\[ x = -\frac{10}{2} + \frac{5}{2} \]
\[ x = -\frac{5}{2} \]

---

### Final Answer

The coordinates of vertex \( R \) are:
\[
\boxed{-\frac{5}{2}, \frac{5}{2}}
\]

Denominator:

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Determine the Line Equation:**
   - Given two points \( A(4,5) \) and \( B(1,2) \).
   
2. **Calculate the Slope (\( m \)):**
   - \( m = (y_2 - y_1)/(x_2 - x_1) = (2 - 5)/(1 - 4) = (-3)/(-3) = 1 \).

3. **Find the Equation of Line AB:**
   - Using point-slope form with point \( A(4,5) \):
     \( y - 5 = 1(x - 4) \)
     Simplify to get \( y = x + 1 \).
   
4. **Calculate Distance from Point C(3,4) to Line AB:**
   - Using the formula for distance from a point (\( x_0,y_0 \)) to line \( ax + by + c = 0 \):
     \( d = |a x_0 + b y_0 + c| / \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} \)
   
   - Rewrite the line equation: \( x - y + 1 = 0 \) => Here, \( a=1 \), \( b=-1 \), \( c=1 \).
   
5. **Plug in Values:**
   - \( d = |1*3 + (-1)*4 + 1| / \sqrt{1^2 + (-1)^2} = |3 - 4 + 1| / √2 = |0| / √2 = 0 \).

6. **Distance from Point A(4,5) to Line BC:**
   - Assume line BC passing through points \( B(1,2) \) and \( C(3,4) \).
   
7. **Calculate the Slope of BC:**
   - \( m = (4-2)/(3-1) = 2/2 = 1 \).

8. **Equation of Line BC:**
   - Using point B(1,2):
     \( y - 2 = 1(x - 1) \)
     => \( y = x + 1 \).
   
9. **Distance from A to BC:**
   - Since both lines AB and BC have the same equation (\( y = x + 1 \)), point A lies on this line.
   - Therefore, distance is zero.

However, since all calculations lead to a zero distance, it's implied that points are collinear. But for triangle height calculation, non-zero distances are expected.

**Final Answer:**
The perpendicular distance (height) from vertex \( A(4,5) \) to the opposite side of the triangle is 0 units.

The angle θ between the two lines with slopes -3 and -1/√3 can be found using the formula:

tanθ = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

Substituting the values:
m₁ = -3, m₂ = -1/√3
= |(-1/√3 - (-3)) / (1 + (-3)*(-1/√3))|
= |(3 - 1/√3) / (1 + √3)|

After simplifying and calculating numerically:
θ ≈ 41.5 degrees.

Answer: The angle between the lines is approximately 41.5°, so θ = 41.5°.

$\boxed{41.5^\circ}$

The equation of the line that is perpendicular to \( X - 7y + 5 = 0 \) and passes through the point with an x-intercept at 5 (i.e., the point (5, 0)) can be found as follows:

1. **Find the slope of the given line**:  
   The original equation can be rewritten in slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \):  
   \( X - 7y + 5 = 0 \) becomes \( y = \frac{1}{7}X + \frac{5}{7} \).  
   So, the slope (\( m \)) is \( \frac{1}{7} \).

2. **Determine the perpendicular slope**:  
   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal. Thus, the new slope is \( -7 \).

3. **Use point-slope form with (5, 0)**:  
   Plugging into \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \):  
   \( y - 0 = -7(X - 5) \), which simplifies to \( y = -7X + 35 \).

4. **Convert back to standard form**:  
   Bringing all terms to one side: \( 7X + y - 35 = 0 \).

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the perpendicular line is \boxed{7x + y - 35 = 0}.

The given equation is \(6x + 4y - 24 = 0\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Convert to Slope-Intercept Form (\(y = mx + b\)):**
   - Start with the original equation:
     \[
     6x + 4y - 24 = 0
     \]
   - Subtract \(6x\) and add 24 to both sides:
     \[
     4y = -6x + 24
     \]
   - Divide each term by 4 to solve for y:
     \[
     y = -\frac{3}{2}x + 6
     \]

2. **Find the X-Intercept:**
   - Set \(y = 0\) and solve for x:
     \[
     6x + 4(0) - 24 = 0 \\
     6x = 24 \\
     x = 4
     \]
   - The x-intercept is at \((4, 0)\).

3. **Find the Y-Intercept:**
   - Set \(x = 0\) and solve for y:
     \[
     6(0) + 4y - 24 = 0 \\
     4y = 24 \\
     y = 6
     \]
   - The y-intercept is at \((0, 6)\).

**Final Answer:**
- **Slope-Intercept Form:** \(y = -\frac{3}{2}x + 6\)
- **X-Intercept:** (4, 0)
- **Y-Intercept:** (0, 6)

The intercept form of the equation \(12x - 9y = 18\) can be found by determining where the line crosses the x-axis and y-axis.

**To find the intercepts:**

1. **X-intercept:** Set \(y = 0\):
   \[
   12x = 18 \implies x = \frac{18}{12} = \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   So, the x-intercept is at \(\left(\frac{3}{2}, 0\right)\).

2. **Y-intercept:** Set \(x = 0\):
   \[
   -9y = 18 \implies y = -2
   \]
   So, the y-intercept is at \((0, -2)\).

**Intercept form of the equation:**
\[
\frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{-2} = 1
\]

Alternatively, simplifying:
\[
\frac{2x}{3} - \frac{y}{2} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**

The intercept form of the equation is \(\boxed{\frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{-2} = 1}\).

To determine the slope of a line passing through two points, I first identify the coordinates of both points.

Next, I calculate the change in y (Δy) by subtracting the y-coordinate of the first point from the y-coordinate of the second point.

Then, I calculate the change in x (Δx) by subtracting the x-coordinate of the first point from the x-coordinate of the second point.

After obtaining both Δy and Δx, I divide Δy by Δx to find the slope of the line.

Finally, I present the calculated slope as the final answer.
</think>

Let's determine the slope (m) of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the coordinates:**
   - Point 1: \((x_1, y_1) = (6, -4)\)
   - Point 2: \((x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)\)

2. **Calculate the change in y (\(\Delta y\)):**
   \[
   \Delta y = y_2 - y_1 = 8 - (-4) = 8 + 4 = 12
   \]

3. **Calculate the change in x (\(\Delta x\)):**
   \[
   \Delta x = x_2 - x_1 = -2 - 6 = -8
   \]

4. **Compute the slope (m):**
   \[
   m = \frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x} = \frac{12}{-8} = -\frac{3}{2}
   \]

### Final Answer:
The slope of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\) is \(\boxed{-\dfrac{3}{2}}\).

\[
\text{slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Given the points \((5, 0)\) and \((9, 0)\):

\[
\text{slope} = \frac{0 - 0}{9 - 5} = \frac{0}{4} = 0
\]

To find the **slope** (gradient) of the line passing through the points \( (7, 2) \) and \( (7, 8) \), we can use the formula for slope:

\[
\text{Slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given coordinates:

\[
\text{Slope} = \frac{8 - 2}{7 - 7} = \frac{6}{0}
\]

Since division by zero is undefined, the slope of this vertical line is **undefined**.

First, I'll determine the slope of the line passing through points (0, 8) and (6, 10). The slope formula is (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1), which gives me (10-8)/(6-0) = 2/6 simplifying to 1/3.

Next, I'll find the equation of this line using the point-slope form. Using point (0, 8), the equation becomes y = (1/3)x + 8.

**Solution:**

We need to find the value of \( x \) on the line passing through points (0, 8) and (6, 10). This new line should be perpendicular to another line that passes through (10, 14) and (x, 26).

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the First Line

The slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

For the points (0, 8) and (6, 10):
\[
m = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line

The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal. So, if \( m_1 \) is the slope of one line, then:
\[
m_2 = -\frac{1}{m_1}
\]
Given that our first line has \( m_1 = \frac{1}{3} \), its perpendicular slope \( m_2 \) will be:
\[
m_2 = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{3}} = -3
\]

### Step 3: Equation of the Perpendicular Line

We'll use the point-slope form to find the equation of the line that passes through (10, 14) with slope \( m_2 = -3 \):
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substituting the known values:
\[
y - 14 = -3(x - 10)
\]
Simplify this to find the equation in slope-intercept form:
\[
y = -3x + 30 + 14 \\
y = -3x + 44
\]

### Step 4: Find \( x \) When \( y = 26 \)

Substitute \( y = 26 \) into the equation:
\[
26 = -3x + 44
\]
Solve for \( x \):
\[
-3x = 26 - 44 \\
-3x = -18 \\
x = \frac{-18}{-3} \\
x = 6
\]

### Final Answer

The value of \( x \) is:
\[
\boxed{6}
\]

The Cartesian coordinates form a quadrilateral when connected in order. Applying the shoelace formula with the given vertices yields an area of 78.5 square units.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **List the Coordinates:** The points are A(-6,7), B(2,9), C(7,-3), and D(-2,0). Assuming they form a quadrilateral in this order without crossing lines.
   
2. **Apply the Shoelace Formula:**
   - Multiply each x-coordinate by the next y-coordinate:
     (-6)*9 + 2*(-3) + 7*0 + (-2)*7 = -54 -6 + 0 -14 = -74
   - Multiply each y-coordinate by the next x-coordinate:
     7*2 + 9*7 + (-3)*(-2) + 0*(-6) = 14 + 63 + 6 + 0 = 83
   
3. **Calculate Area:**
   Subtract the two sums and take half the absolute value.
   |(-74 - 83)| / 2 = 157 / 2 = 78.5

Next, I'll calculate the distance from P to each of the given points using the distance formula. The distance between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is √[(x2 - x1)² + (y2 - y1)²].

So, the distance from P(x, 0) to A(9, 8) is √[(9 - x)² + (8 - 0)²] = √[(9 - x)² + 64]. Similarly, the distance from P(x, 0) to B(5, 6) is √[(5 - x)² + (6 - 0)²] = √[(5 - x)² + 36].

√[(9 - x)² + 64] = √[(5 - x)² + 36]

(9 - x)² + 64 = (5 - x)² + 36

Simplifying both sides:

145 - 18x + x² = 61 - 10x + x²

Subtracting (61 - 10x + x²) from both sides:

84 - 8x = 0

Solving for x:

8x = 84 → x = 10.5

Therefore, the point on the x-axis that is equidistant from (9, 8) and (5, 6) is (10.5, 0).

- The side AC has a slope of \( 1 \).
- The altitude is perpendicular to AC, so its slope is \( -1 \).

- The altitude is perpendicular to AB, so its slope is \( 1 \).
- Using the point-slope form: \( y + 1 = 1(x + 1) \), which simplifies to \( y = x \).

The problem is to find the slope of a line that makes an angle of 60 degrees with the positive Y-axis. 

1. Understand that the angle made with the Y-axis (θ) can be converted to the angle made with the X-axis (φ), where φ = 90° - θ.
2. Calculate φ: φ = 90° - 60° = 30°.
3. The slope (m) is tan(φ): m = tan(30°).
4. Knowing that tan(30°) = 1/√3, the slope is 1/√3.

Answer: 1/sqrt(3)

To determine the slope of the line passing through the points (5, 1) and (6, 0), I first identify the coordinates of each point. Let's denote Point A as (x₁, y₁) = (5, 1) and Point B as (x₂, y₂) = (6, 0).

Next, I'll use the slope formula: 

Slope (m) = (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁)

Substituting the values into the formula:

Slope = (0 - 1)/(6 - 5)  
= (-1)/1  
= -1

Therefore, the slope of the line is -1.

The problem requires finding the slopes of two lines where one slope is twice the other and the tangent of the angle between them is 1/3.

Let m₁ = 2m₂. The formula for tanφ gives:

|(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁m₂)| = 1/3

Substituting m₁=2m₂:

|m₂ - 2m₂| / |1 + 2m₂²| = 1/3 → |m₂|/(1 + 2m₂²) = 1/3

Solving the quadratic equation, we find m₂ can be ±1 or ±0.5.

Thus, the possible slopes are:

- If m₂=1: m₁=2
- If m₂=-1: m₁=-2
- If m₂=0.5: m₁=1
- If m₂=-0.5: m₁=-1

These satisfy both conditions of one slope being twice the other and tanφ = 1/3.

Okay, so I have this problem where I need to find the equation of a line that passes through the point (0,2) and makes an angle of 2π/3 with the positive x-axis. Additionally, I also need to find the equation of a line parallel to this one that intersects the y-axis at some point.

First, let's understand what it means for a line to make an angle of 2π/3 with the positive x-axis. The slope of a line is related to the tangent of the angle it makes with the x-axis. So, if the angle θ is 2π/3 radians (which is 120 degrees), then the slope m should be tan(θ).

Calculating tan(2π/3). Hmm, 2π/3 is in the second quadrant where tangent is negative because sine is positive and cosine is negative there. The reference angle for 2π/3 is π - 2π/3 = π/3. So tan(2π/3) is equal to -tan(π/3). I know that tan(π/3) is √3, so tan(2π/3) should be -√3. Therefore, the slope m of our first line is -√3.

Now, using the point-slope form of a line's equation: y - y1 = m(x - x1). The point given here is (0, 2), which is actually the y-intercept because x=0 there. So, plugging in, we get y - 2 = -√3(x - 0) → y = -√3x + 2.

So that's the equation of the first line: y = -√3x + 2.

Next, I need to find a line parallel to this one. Parallel lines have the same slope, so the new line will also have a slope of -√3. Since it needs to intersect the y-axis at some point, let's denote that point as (0, b). To find the equation of a straight line that makes an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians (which is 120 degrees) with the positive direction of the x-axis in the anticlockwise sense and passes through the point (0, 2):

1. The slope \( m \) of the line is calculated using the tangent of the angle:
   \[
   m = \tan\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\right) = \tan(120^\circ) = -\sqrt{3}
   \]
2. Using the point-slope form of the equation of a line with the given point (0, 2):
   \[
   y - 2 = -\sqrt{3}(x - 0)
   \]
   Simplifying this, we get:
   \[
   y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2
   \]

To find the equation of another line parallel to this one that intersects the negative part of the y-axis:

1. A line parallel to the first will have the same slope \( m = -\sqrt{3} \).
2. To ensure it intersects the negative part of the y-axis, we choose a negative y-intercept (for example, -1):
   \[
   y = -\sqrt{3}x - 1
   \]

Thus, the equations are:
- First line: \( \boxed{y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2} \)
- Second line: \( \boxed{y = -\sqrt{3}x - 1} \)

The problem involves finding a straight line that passes through the point (4,4) with one of its x-intercepts at 13. 

1. **Finding the y-intercept:**
   - The general form of the equation using intercepts is \( \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1 \).
   - Given a=13, substituting into the equation gives \( \frac{x}{13} + \frac{y}{b} = 1 \).

2. **Substituting (4,4) into the equation:**
   - \( \frac{4}{13} + \frac{4}{b} = 1 \)
   - Solving for b:
     \[
     \frac{4}{b} = 1 - \frac{4}{13} = \frac{9}{13}
     \]
     \[
     b = \frac{4 \times 13}{9} = \frac{52}{9}
     \]

3. **Forming the equation:**
   - Substituting a and b back into \( \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1 \):
     \[
     \frac{x}{13} + \frac{9y}{52} = 1
     \]
   - Multiplying through by the least common multiple (52) to eliminate denominators:
     \[
     4x + 9y = 52
     \]

Thus, the equation of the line is:

\boxed{4x + 9y = 52}

To determine the equation of a line in its simplest form when provided with two points, follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Slope (m)**:  
   The slope \( m \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]

2. **Use the Point-Slope Form**:  
   Once you have the slope, use one of the points and the slope to write the equation in point-slope form:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]

3. **Convert to General Form**:  
   Rearrange the equation to the general form \( Ax + By + C = 0 \).

For example, if given two points \( (4, 5) \) and another point determined from the context, follow these steps with those specific points.

Final Answer: {Final Answer: "Equation in reduced form using the given point"}

The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x - 6y -28 = 0 can be calculated using the formula:

Distance = |A*x0 + B*y0 + C| / sqrt(A² + B²)

Here:
- A = 5
- B = -6
- C = -28
- x0 = 5, y0 = -7

Substituting these values into the formula gives:

Numerator: |(5*5) + (-6*-7) + (-28)| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39|

Denominator: sqrt(5² + (-6)^2) = sqrt(25 + 36) = sqrt(61)

So the distance is 39/sqrt(61). To rationalize the denominator, multiply numerator and denominator by sqrt(61):

Distance = (39*sqrt(61)) / 61

The distance between two parallel lines can be calculated using the formula:

\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

For the given lines \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\) and \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\), we have:
- \(C_1 = 12\)
- \(C_2 = 7\)
- \(A = 3\)
- \(B = -4\)

Plugging these into the formula:

\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|12 - 7|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1 \]

The distance between the two parallel lines is 1.

The distance from the point (1,5) to the line 3x -4y -16=0 can be calculated using the formula:

$$
d = \frac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
$$

Where \( A = 3 \), \( B = -4 \), \( C = -16 \), and the point is (x₀, y₀) = (1,5).

Substituting these values:

Numerator: |(3*1) + (-4*5) -16| = |3 -20 -16| = |-33| = 33

Denominator: √(3² + (-4)^2) = √(9+16) = √25 = 5

Distance \( d \): 33 / 5 = 6.6

= |[ (-sqrt(3)/3 + 1) ] / [1 + (sqrt(3)/3)]|

= (12 - 6*sqrt(3)) / 6
= Simplify numerator: factor out 6: 6*(2 - sqrt(3))/6 = 2 - sqrt(3).

The equation of the line parallel to \(5x - 6y = C\) that passes through \((-4, 5)\) is found by ensuring the same coefficients for \(x\) and \(y\), then solving for the constant term using the point.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand Parallel Lines:** Two lines are parallel if they have the same slope. In standard form (\(Ax + By = C\)), this means their \(A\) and \(B\) coefficients are proportional, but here since we're maintaining the exact proportions (same ratio), we can use the same \(A\) and \(B\).

2. **Use Point to Find Constant Term:** The given line is \(5x - 6y = C'\). To find \(C'\) for the new line passing through \((-4,5)\):

   Substitute \(x = -4\) and \(y = 5\) into \(5x - 6y + K = 0\), solving for \(K\):
   
   \[
   5(-4) - 6(5) + K = 0 \\
   -20 - 30 + K = 0 \\
   -50 + K = 0 \\
   K = 50
   \]
   
3. **Form the Equation:** Plugging \(K\) back into the standard form gives:
   
   \[
   5x - 6y + 50 = 0
   \]

**Answer:**

The equation of the desired line is:

\[5x - 6y + 50 = 0\]

We have two equations:
1. \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\)
2. \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\)

### Step 2: Check for Parallelism
Two lines are parallel if their slopes are equal. 

- The slope of the first line can be found by converting it to the slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
  
  \[
  9x + 6y - 7 = 0 \\
  6y = -9x + 7 \\
  y = -\frac{3}{2}x + \frac{7}{6}
  \]
  
  So, the slope (\(m\)) is \(-\frac{3}{2}\).

- For the second line:
  
  \[
  3x + 2y + 6 = 0 \\
  2y = -3x - 6 \\
  y = -\frac{3}{2}x - 3
  \]
  
  The slope is also \(-\frac{3}{2}\).

Since both lines have the same slope, they are indeed parallel.

### Step 3: Find a Coincident Line
A line that coincides with \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\) will be identical in form but different in constants. To find such a line, we can modify the constant term while keeping the coefficients of \(x\) and \(y\) proportional.

Let's assume the new line is:
\[
9x + 6y + C = 0
\]
Here, \(C\) is a constant that ensures parallelism but different position.

Since it should be coincident with \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\), we can find \(C\) by scaling the second equation appropriately. However, to make them identical in form:

Multiply the entire equation \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\) by 3:
\[
9x + 6y + 18 = 0
\]

Thus, the coincident line is:
\[
9x + 6y + 18 = 0
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the line parallel to \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\) and coinciding with \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\) is:

\[
\boxed{9x + 6y + 18 = 0}
\]
